Coronas and Callias type operators in coarse geometry
Ulrich Bunke and Matthias Ludewig

Abstract
We interpret the coarse symbol and index class of a Callias type Dirac operator D̸ + Ψ \not{D}+\Psi on a manifold M M as a pairing between the coarse symbol and index classes associated to D̸ \not{D} and K-theory classes of the coarse corona of M M or M M itself determined by Ψ \Psi . Local positivity of D̸ \not{D} and local invertibility of Ψ \Psi are incorporated in terms of support conditions on the K K -theoretic level.



1
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to embed
the index theory of Callias type Dirac operators on complete Riemannian manifolds into the coarse homotopy theory developed in
[
BE20b
,
BE20a
]
. Our main innovation is
Theorem

4.32
which expresses the index and symbol of Callias type operators
in terms of the coarse corona and symbol pairings introduced
Definition

3.35
and

Definition

3.40
.

One of the achievements of the present paper is to provide a
K
-theoretic interpretation of Callias type Dirac operators beyond the Fredholm index case.
As an application of the theory we
discuss a version of the partitioned manifold index theorem
[
Roe88
]
and its multipartitioned generalization
[
Sie12
,
Zei17
,
SZ18
]
.
New aspects are that we allow multipartitions along non-compact submanifolds, work with the refined symbol classes in local
K
-homology, and that we take the support conditions into account.

In the present paper we work in the setup of coarse homotopy theory developed in
[
BE20b
,
BE20a
]

which is based on the categories
\mathbf{BC}
and
\mathbf{UBC}
of bornological coarse and uniform bornological coarse spaces and the
KU
-module-valued coarse
K
-homology functor

K\mathscr{X}:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)

from
[
BE23
]
.
In order to keep this paper selfcontained, we review the relevant definitions and results of this theory.
The wish to present the setup in a coherent way and to give at least complete definitions
accounts for most of the length of this paper.

Coarse index theory with supports.

As observed by J. Roe
[
Roe93
]
the basic global
K
-theoretic invariant of a generalized Dirac operator
\not{D}
on a complete Riemannian manifold
M

is its coarse index class
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D})
in the coarse
K
-homology
K\mathscr{X}(M)
(see
Definition

2.56
and
Definition

4.7
). This index class detects the invertibility of
\not{D}

on the natural
L^{2}
-space, but also retains some coarsely local information, e.g., on which parts of
M
the zero order term in the Weizenboeck formula for
\not{D}
is uniformly positive
[
Roe16
]
.

Classically, the local information on
\not{D}
is homotopy theoretically encoded by the analytic symbol class

\sigma^{\mathrm{an}}(\not{D})
in the locally finite analytic
K
-homology
K^{\mathrm{an}}(M)\simeq\mathrm{KK}(C_{0}(M),{\mathbb{C}})
of
M
(see
Definition

3.29
and
Definition

4.15
, but note that in the present paper we prefer to work with
E
-theory).
The analytic symbol class can, e.g., be paired with compactly supported
K
-theory classes of
M
.
If one realizes
KK
-theory classes by Kasparov modules
[
Kas88
]
, then
the results of these pairings can be interpreted
in terms of indices of Fredholm operators constructed from
\not{D}
, see
Example

1.1
. But (somewhat surprisingly) one can also recover the coarse index
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D})

from the symbol class
\sigma^{\mathrm{an}}(\not{D})
, see
Remark

4.16
.

In order to capture the symbol of
\not{D}
within the coarse
K
-homology calculus,
in
[
Buna
,
BE17
]
we proposed to
consider a refined symbol class
\sigma(\not{D})
in the local
K
-homology
K^{\mathscr{X}}(M)
(see
Definition

3.25
and
Definition

4.14
). There is a natural transformation
a:K^{\mathscr{X}}\to K\mathscr{X}
(see (
3.11
)) of local homology theories called the index map
such that

a_{M}(\sigma(\not{D}))=\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D})\ .

(1.1)

The classical symbol class in analytic
K
-homology can be recovered in terms of the natural Paschke duality transformation of local homology theories

p:K^{\mathscr{X}}\to K^{\mathrm{an}}
(see (
3.16
)) by

p_{M}(\sigma(\not{D}))=\sigma^{\mathrm{an}}(\not{D})\ .

The advantage of considering the symbol
\sigma(\not{D})
instead of
\sigma^{\mathrm{an}}(\not{D})
is that one can incorporate local positivity of
\not{D}

geometrically in terms of additional support conditions

[
Buna
,
BE17
]
.
In coarse geometry, the right notion of a subset is a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
, i.e., a filtered family of subsets which is closed under forming coarse thickenings.
Given such a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
on
M
one can define the local
K
-homology
K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(M)
(see
Definition

3.26
)
with support on
{\mathcal{Y}}
and the coarse
K
-homology
K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}K\mathscr{X}(Y)
of
{\mathcal{Y}}
.
The index map then refines to a map

a_{M,{\mathcal{Y}}}:K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(M)\to K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Y}}),

see (
3.12
).

Assume now that there exists a member
Y
of
{\mathcal{Y}}
such that
\not{D}^{2}
is positive on
M\setminus Y
in the sense that the
quadratic form determined by
\smash{\not{D}^{2}}
on smooth sections which are compactly supported on
M\setminus\bar{Y}
is positive.
In
Definition

4.14
,
we define a refined symbol class
\sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(\not{D})
in
K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(M)
such that

{a_{M,{\mathcal{Y}}}(\sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(\not{D}))=\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D},\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}})}

in
K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Y}})
.

For example, if
\not{D}
is positive on all of
M
, then
\not{D}
is invertible and
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D})=0
.
Taking
{\mathcal{Y}}={\{\emptyset\}}
, the class

\smash{\sigma_{{\{\emptyset\}}}}(\not{D})
in
\smash{K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\{\emptyset\}}}(M)}
(called the
\rho
-invariant) captures the reason for the equality
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D})=0
in a homotopy theoretic manner.

The local
K
-homology
K^{\mathscr{X}}
(with supports) and the index map
a
are instances of general constructions
which can be performed with arbitrary coarse homology theories, see
Definition

2.36
and
Definition

2.40
. In the present paper we just applied them to the coarse
K
-homology theory
K\mathscr{X}
. These constructions are functorial with respect to natural transformations of coarse homology theories, an observation which waits to be exploited in the future. Furthermore, the index map is a natural transformation of local homology theories (say on the spectrum level) and therefore automatically compatible with Mayer-Vietoris boundary maps.
In contrast, the construction of the classical analytic index map

a_{M}^{\mathrm{an}}:K_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(M)\to K\mathscr{X}_{*}(M)

(see, e.g.,
[
HR95
, Sec. 5 & 6]
) is based on complicated analytic arguments using specific models of
K_{*}^{\mathrm{an}}(M)
and a group-level Paschke duality isomorphism
which is not obviously liftable to the spectrum level in a functorial manner.

Callias type operators.

By
Definition

4.20
a Callias type operator is a generalized Dirac operator of the form
\not{D}+\Psi
obtained from
a generalized Dirac operator
\not{D}
by adding a potential
\Psi
such that
\not{D}\Psi+\Psi\not{D}
is zero order (see
Definition

4.19
).
The relevant index theoretic information on
\not{D}+\Psi
is concentrated on the subset of
M
on which the potential is not positive in the sense that
\Psi^{2}
dominates
\not{D}\Psi+\Psi\not{D}
(see
Definition

4.21
).

The classical condition is that the potential is positive outside of a compact subset of
M
which turns the Callias type operator into a Fredholm operator. Partial positivity as considered in the present paper was already studied in
[
GHM22
]
.

We consider a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
such that the potential is positive away from
{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Then
\not{D}+\Psi
is positive away from
{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Since this positivity is caused by the zero order term of
\not{D}+\Psi
in
Definition

4.24
we can define
a refined symbol class with support
\sigma(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}})
in
K^{\mathscr{X}}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}K^{\mathscr{X}}(Y)
.

If
\not{D}
was already positive away from a big family
{\mathcal{Z}}

and
\Psi
is asymptotically constant away from
{\mathcal{Z}}
, then
\not{D}+\Psi
is positive away from
{\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}}
with index
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})
in
K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})
, and
we define a refined symbol class

\sigma_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}})
in
K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}})
.

We want to interpret the symbol class
\sigma_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}})
and the coarse index class

\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})=a_{M,{{\mathcal{Z}}}}(\sigma_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}}))

in terms of
pairings between the symbol class
\sigma_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\not{D})
or the index class
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D},\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Z}})
with suitable
K
-theory classes determined by the potential
\Psi
.

The big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
gives rise to the uniform
{\mathcal{Y}}
-corona
\partial_{u}^{{\mathcal{Y}}}M
, a compact topological space with the potential to capture the behaviour of
\Psi
at
\infty
(see
Definition

3.16
).
As part of the coarse
K
-homology calculus we introduce the coarse corona pairing
Definition

3.35

-\cap^{\mathscr{X}}-:{K(\partial^{{\mathcal{Y}}}M)}\times K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Z}})\to\Sigma K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})

and
the coarse symbol pairing
Definition

3.40

-\cap^{\mathscr{X}\sigma}-:K({\mathcal{Y}})\times K_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\mathscr{X}}(M)\to K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}})\ ,

involving the topological
K
-theory of the coarse corona
\partial^{{\mathcal{Y}}}X
(
Definition

3.2
) and of
{\mathcal{Y}}
.
Both pairings are components of extra-natural transformations, see
Remark

3.36
.

Under the assumptions made above, the potential
\Psi
gives naturally rise to a class
[\Psi]
in
K({\mathcal{Y}})
. Our main result is
Theorem

4.32
, which states that under suitable conditions on
\not{D}
and
\Psi
, one has

\sigma_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}})=[\Psi]\cap^{\mathscr{X}\sigma}\sigma_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\not{D})

in
K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}})
.
It provides the interpretation of the symbol class of the Callias type operator in terms of
the coarse
K
-homology calculus.

We now consider the boundary map

\partial:K(\partial_{u}^{{\mathcal{Y}}}M)\to\Sigma K({\mathcal{Y}})

from (
3.10
).
We assume that there exists a class
p
in
K(\partial_{u}^{{\mathcal{Y}}}M)
with
\partial p=[\Psi]
.
Then by the compatibility of
\partial
with the index map shown in
Proposition

3.43

we get

\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})=c^{*}p\cap^{\mathscr{X}}\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D},\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Z}})

(1.2)

in
K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})
, where
c
is the comparison map (
3.9
).
As a consequence of (
1.2
), the assumption
[\Psi]=\partial p
implies the non-obvious fact that the coarse index

\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})
only depends on
\not{D}
via its coarse index
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D},\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Z}})
, instead of the finer information contained it its symbol class
\sigma_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(\not{D})
.

Example 1.1
.

We assume that

M
is connected and that
{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{B}}
is the family of bounded subsets. Then we have

K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{B}})\simeq KU

and under this identification
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D}+\Psi,\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{B}})
is the Fredholm index
of
\not{D}+\Psi
. The class
[\Psi]
in
K_{{\mathcal{B}}}(M)
is a compactly supported
K
-theory class of
M
and the Fredholm index
of
\not{D}+\Psi
is the result of the pairing of
\sigma^{\mathrm{an}}(\not{D})
with the class
[\Psi]
.

In this case

\partial^{{\mathcal{B}}}M
is the Higson corona
\partial_{h}M
of
M
. If
[\Psi]=\partial p
for some class
p
in
K(\partial_{h}M)
, then the interpretation of the Fredholm index of
\not{D}+\Psi
in terms of a pairing between the
K
-theory
K(\partial_{h}M)
of
the Higson corona and symbol
\sigma^{\mathrm{an}}(\not{D})
is known from

[
Bun95
]
.

\blacksquare

Structure of the paper.

In the first sections we will recall the main definitions and constructions
of the coarse homotopy calculus,
K
-theory of
C^{*}
-algebras and categories, and coarse index theory of Dirac operators.
Prerequisites for reading this paper are basic homotopy theoretic language (e.g., elements of the theory of stable
\infty
-categories), basic
C^{*}
-algebra theory (continuous function calculus, maximal tensor product, Gelfand duality) and some global analysis (Riemannian manifold and generalized Dirac operators).

In
Section

2.1
we recall from
[
BE20b
,
BE20a
]
the definitions of the categories
\mathbf{BC}
of bornological coarse spaces and
\mathbf{UBC}
of uniform bornological coarse spaces and the cone functor
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{BC}
.

In
Section

2.2
we recall from
[
BE20b
]
the notion of a coarse homology theory in general and some basic calculations.

In
Section

2.3
we recall from
[
BE20a
]
and
[
BE20b
]
the notions of a local and locally finite homology theory.
We explain the construction of the local homology theory associated to a coarse homology theory and the index map.
We further introduce the local homology theory with supports associated to a coarse homology theory.

In
Section

2.4
we give a descriptive presentation
E
- and
K
-theory via universal properties
for (graded)
C^{*}
-algebras and categories in the style of
[
Bun23
,
BD24
]
.

Section

2.5
is devoted to the construction of the coarse
K
-homology functor
K\mathscr{X}:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
in terms of the
C^{*}
-categories of controlled Hilbert spaces taken from
[
BE20b
]
and
[
BE23
]
.

In
Section

3.1
and
Section

3.2
we introduce various
C^{*}
-algebras of functions associated to
a big family by putting support conditions on the functions themselves or their variation.
We introduce the corona associated to a pair of big families and show a crucial technical commutator estimate in
Lemma

3.9
.

In
Section

3.3
we define the analytic locally finite
K
-homology functor
K^{\mathrm{an}}(-):=\mathrm{E}(C_{0}(-),{\mathbb{C}})

and verify that it is a locally finite local homology theory.
We then recall from
[
QR10
,
BELa
]

the Paschke transformation
p_{M}:K^{\mathscr{X}}(M)\to K^{\mathrm{an}}(M)
and provide conditions on
M
ensuring that
p_{M}
it is an equivalence. We also explain the factorization of the index map over a map
K^{\mathrm{an}}(M)\to K\mathscr{X}(M)

which allows to recover the coarse index of
\not{D}
from its analytic symbol in
Remark

4.16
.

In Sections
3.4
and
3.5
, we construct the coarse corona pairing and the coarse symbol pairing and state and verify their formal functorial properties.

In
Section

4.1
we recall from
[
BE17
]
the details of the construction of coarse index
\mathrm{ind}\mathscr{X}(\not{D},\mathrm{on}\,{\mathcal{Y}})
in
K\mathscr{X}({\mathcal{Y}})
of a Dirac operator which is positive away from a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
. We state the suspension theorem (identifying the index operators on products
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes M
) and the coarse relative index theorem expressing the locality of the coarse index.

In
Section

4.2
we first recall from
[
Buna
]
how to capture symbols of Dirac operators which are positive away from
{\mathcal{Y}}
as classes
\sigma_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(\not{D})
in
K^{\mathscr{X}}_{{\mathcal{Y}}}(M)
.

The main result of the present paper is
Theorem

4.32
stated in
Section

4.3
which identifies the symbol of the Callias type operator
\not{D}+\Psi
with support on
{\mathcal{Y}}
as the coarse symbol paring
[\Psi]\cap^{\mathscr{X}\sigma}\sigma(\not{D})
.

In
Section

4.5
we discuss the coarse version of the Dirac-goes-to-Dirac principle.

This principle
is then used in
Section

4.6
in the discussion of the relation between our pairings and Mayer-Vietoris boundary maps leading to a version of the partitioned manifold theorem.

In
Section

4.7
we derive a version of the multipartitioned manifold index theorem by an interated application of the partitioned manifold theorem.

Finally, in
Section

4.8

we argue that the slant products considered in
[
EWZ22
]
can be derived by specializing our pairings, and that their basic properties
can be derived from general facts about these pairings.

Most of the material discussed in this paper has an immediate generalization to the equivariant case for discrete group actions.
We decided not to formulate everything in this generality in order to keep the complexity of the paper reasonable.

Acknowledgement: The authors were supported by the SFB 1085 (Higher Invariants) funded by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG).

2
Coarse cohomology theories

2.1
Uniform bornological coarse spaces and cones

In this section we recall the notions of bornological coarse spaces, uniform bornological coarse spaces, topological bornological spaces and the cone construction
[
BE20b
,
BEKW20
]
. We just mention that coarse geometry has been invented by J. Roe
[
Roe90
,
Roe03
]
and refer to the references above for further links to the literature on coarse geometry and attributions.

Let
X
be a set.

Definition 2.1
.

1.

A bornology on
X
is a subset
{\mathcal{B}}
of the power set of
X
which covers
X
and is closed under forming
subsets and finite unions.

2.

A coarse structure on
X
is a subset
{\mathcal{C}}
of the power set of
X\times X
which is closed under forming subsets, finite unions, compositions
(U,U^{\prime})\mapsto U\circ U
(in the sense of relations), the flip of
X\times X
, and which contains the diagonal
{\mathtt{diag}}(X)
of
X
.

3.

A uniform structure on
X
is a subset
{\mathcal{U}}
of the power set of
X\times X
of subsets
U
with
{\mathtt{diag}}(X)\subseteq U
which is closed under forming supersets, finite intersections, compositions, the flip, and which has the property that for any
U
in
{\mathcal{U}}
there exists a
V
in
{\mathcal{U}}
with
V\circ V\subseteq U
.

For a subset
Z
of
X
and a subset
U
(called an entourage) of
X\times X
we let

U[Z]:=\{x\in X\mid\exists z\in Z:(x,z)\in U\}

(2.1)

denote the
U
-thickening of
Z
.

Let
X
be a set with a coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}
.
The correct notion of a subset in coarse geometry
is the notion of a big family.

Definition 2.2
.

A big family is a nonempty subset
{\mathcal{Y}}
of the power set of
X
which is closed under taking subsets, finite unions and
U
-thickenings for all
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}
.

We consider a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
as a partially ordered set with respect to the inclusion relation.
Because
{\mathcal{Y}}
is closed under taking finite unions, this partially ordered set is filtered.

Remark 2.3
.

In the literature on bornological coarse spaces with the basic reference
[
BE20b
]
, a big family on
X
is a family of subsets
(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
indexed by a filtered poset
I
such that
i\leq j
implies
Y_{i}\subseteq Y_{j}
and such that for every
i
in
I
and
U
in
{\mathcal{C}}
there exists
i^{\prime}
in
I
with
U[Y_{i}]\subseteq Y_{i^{\prime}}
.
In this convention the notion introduced in
Definition

2.2
is the special case of a selfindexing big family
which is in addition complete (this is the closedness under taking subsets).

Every big family
(Y_{i})_{i\in I}
in the sense of
[
BE20b
]
generates a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}:=\{Y\subseteq X\mid\exists i\in I:Y\subseteq Y_{i}\}
as defined in
Definition

2.2
.
The canonical map
I\to{\mathcal{Y}}
,
i\mapsto Y_{i}
of partially ordered sets is cofinal.

One can define an equivalence relation on the collection of big families in the sense of
[
BE20b
]

such that
(Y_{i})_{i\in I}\sim(Y^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}
if and only if the associated big families in the sense of
Definition

2.2
satisfy
{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
.

Since the constructions with big families occuring in the present paper only depend on
the equivalence classes
it suffices to work with complete selfindexing big families
as introduced in
Definition

2.2
.
\blacksquare

Example 2.4
.

If
Y
is a subset of
X
, then

\{Y\}:=\{Z\subseteq X\mid\exists U\in{\mathcal{C}}:Z\subseteq U[Y]\}.

(2.2)

is called the big family generated by
Y
.
If
{\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}
are big families in
X
, then we define the big families

{\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}}:=\{Y\cap Z\mid Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\penalty\ \&\penalty\ Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}\}

(2.3)

and

{\mathcal{Y}}\cup{\mathcal{Z}}:=\{W\subseteq X\mid\exists Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\&Z\in{\mathcal{Z}}:W\subseteq Y\cup Z\}

(2.4)

in
X
.

If
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a big family in
X
and
{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
is a big family in
X^{\prime}
, then we define the big family

{\mathcal{Y}}\times{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}:=\{Z\subseteq X\times X^{\prime}\mid\exists Y\in{\mathcal{Y}},\penalty\ Y^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{Y}}:Z\subseteq Y\times Y^{\prime}\}

(2.5)

in
X\times X^{\prime}
.
In order to simplify the notation we will set
X\times{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}:=\{X\}\times{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}
.

If
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a big family in
X
and
Z\subseteq X
is a subset equipped with the induced coarse structure, then we define the induced big family

Z\cap{\mathcal{Y}}:=\{Z\cap Y\mid Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}\}

(2.6)

in
Z
.
\blacksquare

A big family on a topological space
X
is a family of subsets
{\mathcal{Y}}

which is closed under taking subsets and finite unions and has property that for every
Y
in
{\mathcal{Y}}
there exists
Y^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{Y}}
which contains an open neighbourhood of
\bar{Y}
.

We consider the following compatibility relations between bornologies, coarse structures and uniform structures.

Definition 2.5
.

1.

A uniform structure
{\mathcal{U}}
is compatible with a coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}
if
{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{U}}\not=\emptyset
.

2.

A bornology is
{\mathcal{B}}
compatible with a coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}
if it is a big family.

3.

A bornology
{\mathcal{B}}
is compatible with a topology on
X
if it is a big family (in the topological sense).

Note that a compatible bornology (in the coarse or topological sense) is simply a big family which covers the whole space.

We consider the following conditions on maps
f:X\to Y
between sets equipped with (some) of the above structures.

Definition 2.6
.

1.

f
is proper if
f^{-1}({\mathcal{B}}_{Y})\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{X}
.

2.

f
is bornological, if
f({\mathcal{B}}_{X})\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}
.

3.

f
is controlled if
f({\mathcal{C}}_{X})\subseteq{\mathcal{C}}_{Y}
.

4.

f
is uniform if
f^{-1}({\mathcal{U}}_{Y})\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}_{X}
.

Example 2.7
.

Let
X
und
X^{\prime}
be sets with coarse structures and
f:X^{\prime}\to X
be a controlled map.
If
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a big family on
X
, then generalizing (
2.6
) we define the induced big family

f^{-1}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\{Z^{\prime}\subseteq X^{\prime}\mid\exists Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}:Z^{\prime}\subseteq f^{-1}(Y)\}

in
X^{\prime}
.
For a subset
Y
of
X
we have
\{f^{-1}(Y)\}\subseteq f^{-1}(\{Y\})
, but this inclusion is in general not an equality.

\blacksquare

We will consider the following categories:

Definition 2.8
.

1.

\mathbf{BC}
(bornological coarse spaces): Sets with coarse structures and compatible bornologies and controlled and proper maps.

2.

\mathbf{UBC}
(uniform bornological coarse spaces): Sets with coarse structures, compatible bornologies and uniform structures and controlled, uniform and proper maps.

3.

\mathbf{TB}
(topological bornological spaces): Topological spaces with compatible bornological structures and proper continuous maps.

Recall that a uniform structure
{\mathcal{U}}
on a set
X
induces a topology on
X
generated by the subsets
U[x]

for all
x
in
X
and
U
in
{\mathcal{U}}
.
We have obvious forgetful functors

\iota:\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{BC}\ ,\quad\tau:\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{TB}

which we will often omit from the notation.

Example 2.9
.

A (generalized, i.e., we allow
d(x,y)=\infty
) metric space
(X,d)
presents a uniform bornological coarse space with the following structures:

1.

{\mathcal{B}}
consists of the subsets which can be covered by finitely many metric balls.

2.

{\mathcal{C}}
is consists of all subsets which are contained a metric entourage

U_{r}:=\{(x,y)\in X\times X\mid d(x,y)\leq r\}

(2.7)

for some
r
in
[0,\infty)
.

3.

{\mathcal{U}}
consists of subsets containing a metric entourage
U_{r}
for some
r\in(0,\infty)
(note that
0
is excluded here).

A Lipschitz continuous map between metric spaces is uniform and controlled.
\blacksquare

Example 2.10
.

Let
{\mathbf{Top}}^{{lc}}
denote the category of locally compact topological spaces and proper continuous maps.
We have a fully faithful functor
{\mathbf{Top}}^{{lc}}\to\mathbf{TB}
which equips a locally compact space with the bornology of relatively compact subsets.

We have a functor
{\mathbf{Top}}_{\mathrm{mc}}\to\mathbf{UBC}
from metrizable compact topological spaces to uniform bornological coarse spaces which equips a metrizable compact topological space with the maximal coarse and bornological structures and
the canonical uniform structure (induced by any choice of metric on
X
).

\blacksquare

The category
\mathbf{BC}
has a symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
such that

X\otimes Y
has the following description:

1.

The underlying set of
X\otimes Y
is
X\times Y
.

2.

An entourage of
X\times Y
is coarse for
X\otimes Y
if it is contained a product of entourages of
X
and
Y
.

3.

A subset of
X\times Y
is bounded if it is contained in a product of bounded subsets of
X
and
Y
.

Similarly the category
\mathbf{UBC}
has a symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
such that the underlying bornological coarse space of
X\otimes Y
is as above and the uniform structure consists of entourages
which contain products of uniform entourages of
X
and
Y
.
Finally, also
\mathbf{TB}
has a symmetric monoidal structure given by the cartesian structure on the underlying topological spaces and the product bornology as above.

Definition 2.11
.

The cone functor

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{BC}

(2.8)

is defined as follows:

1.

objects: Let
X
be in
\mathbf{UBC}
:

(a)

The underlying set of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
is
{\mathbb{R}}\times X
.

(b)

The underlying bornology of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
is the one of
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
(where
{\mathbb{R}}
is considered as a metric space).

(c)

The coarse structure of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
consists of those entourages
U
of the coarse product
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
which have that property that for every uniform entourage
V
of
X
there exists
r
in
{\mathbb{R}}
such that

((x,t),(x^{\prime},t^{\prime}))\in U
with
\min(t,t^{\prime})\geq r
implies
(x,x^{\prime})\in V
.

2.

morphisms:
A map
f:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{UBC}
induces a map

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(f):{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(Y)
given by
(t,x)\mapsto(t,f(x))
.

The cone boundary

\partial^{\mathrm{cone}}:{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\to\iota({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X)

(2.9)

is given by the identity of underlying sets.
Observe that in general the product uniform structure on
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
is not compatible with the coarse structure of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
.

The following notions will be relevant in the discussion of Mayer-Vietoris sequences for coarse or local homology theories.
Let
X
be a bornological coarse space and
Y,Z
be two subsets.

Definition 2.12
.

The pair
(Y,Z)
is said to be coarsely excisive if for every coarse entourage
U
if
X
there exists a coarse entourage
V
of
X
such that

U[Y]\cap U[Z]\subseteq V[Y\cap Z]\ .

Equivalenty, the pair
(Y,Z)
is coarsely excisive iff
\{Y\}\cap\{Z\}
is equivalent to
\{Y\cap Z\}
.

Assume now that
X
is a uniform space with uniform structure
{\mathcal{U}}
.

Definition 2.13
.

We say that
(Y,Z)
is uniformly excisive
[
BE20a
, Def. 3.3]
if there exists

U
in
{\mathcal{U}}
and a function
\kappa:\{V\subseteq X\times X\mid V\subseteq U\}\to\mathcal{P}(X\times X)
(the power set of
X\times X
) such that

1.

V\subseteq V^{\prime}\subseteq U
implies
\kappa(V)\subseteq\kappa(V^{\prime})
,

2.

for every
V
in
{\mathcal{U}}

there exists
W
in
{\mathcal{U}}
with
W\subseteq U
and
\kappa(W)\subseteq V
,

3.

for every
W
in
{\mathcal{U}}
with
W\subseteq U
we have

W[Y]\cap W[Z]\subseteq\kappa(W)[Y\cap Z]\ .

If
X
is a uniform bornological coarse space, then
by
[
BEKW20
, Lem. 9.26]
the cone functor sends pairs
(Y,Z)
of subsets which a coarsely and uniformly
excisive to coarsely excisive pairs.

Example 2.14
.

A typical example of coarsely (or uniformly) excisive decomposition is the decomposition
((-\infty,0]\times X,[0,\infty)\times X)
of
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
for a coarse (or uniform) space
X
.

If
X
is presented by a path metric space, then any closed decomposition
(Y,Z)
is coarsely and uniformly excisive.

In contrast, the picture below shows a subset of the plane together with a non-coarsely excisive pair of subsets.
Y
Z

Note that this subset is not a path metric space with the restricted metric.

\blacksquare

2.2
Coarse homology theories

In this section we recall from
[
BE20b
,
BEKW20
]
the notion of a coarse homology theory with values in a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
(e.g., the category of spectra
\mathbf{Sp}
or the category of
KU
-modules
{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
).

Before we can state the axioms we need to recall some further notions from coarse geometry and introduce some notation.

Let
X,Y
be coarse spaces and
f,g:X\to Y
be two controlled maps.

Definition 2.15
.

We say that
f
and
g
are close if the map

f\sqcup g:\{0,1\}\otimes X\to Y\ ,\quad(0,x)\mapsto f(x)\ ,\quad(1,x)\mapsto g(x)

is controlled.

Here
\{0,1\}
has the maximal coarse structure.

Definition 2.16
.

A bornological coarse space
X
is called flasque if it admits a
selfmap
f:X\to X
(called a witness of flasqueness) with the following properties:

1.

f
is close to
{\mathtt{id}}_{X}

2.

f
is non-expanding in the sense that for every coarse enourage
U
of
X
also
\bigcup_{n\in{\rm Nature}}(f^{n}\times f^{n})(U)
is a coarse entourage

3.

f
is shifting in the sense that for every bounded subset
B
there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
with
B\cap f^{n}(X)=\emptyset
.

Example 2.17
.

If
X
is a bornological coarse space and
[0,\infty)
has the bornological coarse structure induced by the metric, then

[0,\infty)\otimes X
is flasque with witness of flasqueness
f:X\to X
given by
f(t,x):=(t+1,x)
.
\blacksquare

We consider a functor

E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}\ .

If
{\mathcal{Y}}
is a big family on
X
in
\mathbf{BC}
(see
Section

2.1
), then we define

E({\mathcal{Y}}):=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}E(Y)\ ,

(2.10)

where we equip the members of
{\mathcal{Y}}
with the bornological coarse structures induced from
X
.

Definition 2.18
.

The functor
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is called a coarse homology theory if it has
the following properties:

1.

Coarse invariance:
The projection
\{0,1\}\otimes X\to X
induces an equivalence
E(\{0,1\}\otimes X)\to E(X)
for every
X
in
\mathbf{BC}
.

2.

Excision: For every
X
in
\mathbf{BC}
each and each pair
({\mathcal{Y}},Z)
of a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
and a subset
Z
of
X
such that
X=Y\cup Z
for some
Y
in
{\mathcal{Y}}
(i.e., a complementary pair), we have a push-out square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 22.83333pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.83333pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}}\cap Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.59721pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.59721pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.2361pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.61806pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.83333pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.83333pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

3.

Vanishing on flasques:
E(X)\simeq 0
if
X
is flasque.

4.

U
-continuity: For every
X
in
\mathbf{BC}
the canonical morphism

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}}E(X_{U})\to E(X)

is an equivalence, where
X_{U}
is the bornological coarse space derived from
X
by replacing the coarse structure by the smaller coarse structure generated by
U
.

Remark 2.19
.

If
Y
is a subset of
X
, then we have a map
E(Y)\to E(\{Y\})
.
If
E
is coarsely invariant, then this map is an equivalence.

\blacksquare

Remark 2.20
.

Coarse invariance of
E
is equivalent to the condition that for every two maps
f,g:X\to Y
in
\mathbf{BC}
which are close to each other we have
E(f)\simeq E(g)
.
\blacksquare

Remark 2.21
.

For a more symmetric version of excision let
{\mathcal{Z}}
be a big family which is complementary to
{\mathcal{Y}}
in the sense that there exists a member
Z
of
{\mathcal{Z}}
such that
({\mathcal{Y}},Z)
is a complementary pair. If
E
is a coarse homology theory, then we also have a push-out square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 21.00696pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-21.00696pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.48611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 46.48611pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.40973pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.61806pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.00696pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 45.00696pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(2.11)

If
(Y,Z)
is a coarsely excisive decomposition of
X
, then by
[
BE20b
, Lem. 3.41]
have a push-out square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Y\cap Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Z)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(X)\ .}

\blacksquare

Remark 2.22
.

In
[
BE20b
]
we have constructed a universal
coarse homology theory

\mathrm{Yo}^{s}:\mathbf{BC}\to\mathbf{Sp}\mathscr{X}

(2.12)

to a certain presentable stable infinity category
\mathbf{Sp}\mathscr{X}
such that for every cocomplete stable
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}

the restriction

(\mathrm{Yo}^{s})^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathbf{Sp}\mathscr{X},{\mathbf{D}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathbf{BC},{\mathbf{D}})

is an equivalence onto the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathbf{BC},{\mathbf{D}})
of coarse homology theories.
In particular, every coarse homology theory
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
has an essentially unique colimit-preserving factorization

\textstyle{\mathbf{BC}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Yo}^{s}}
\scriptstyle{E}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Sp}\mathscr{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}

indicated by the dotted
arrow and denoted by the same symbol.
Since the
\mathbf{Sp}\mathscr{X}
plays a role analogous to the category of spectra in classical homotopy theory it is called the category of coarse spectra.

\blacksquare

Example 2.23
.

A basic consequence of the axioms is a canonical equivalence

\Sigma E(-)\simeq E({\mathbb{R}}\otimes-):\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}\ .

(2.13)

To this end we consider the coarsely excisive decomposition of
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
from
Example

2.14
and get by specializing (
2.21
) a push-out square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 37.34715pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.40277pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.34715pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 61.34715pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E([0,\infty)\otimes X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 95.91655pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-37.34715pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E((-\infty,0]\otimes X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.83324pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.83324pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

We now use that
[0,\infty)\otimes X
and
(-\infty,0]\otimes X
are flasque by
Example

2.17
in order to see that the lower left and upper right corners of this square vanish. This provides an equivalence of the lower right corner of the square with the suspension of the upper left corner.
\blacksquare

A coarse homology theory can have the following additional properties:

1.

Continuity: For every
X
in
\mathbf{BC}
the canonical map

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{Z\in\mathrm{LF}(X)}E(Z)\to E(X)

is an equivalence, where
\mathrm{LF}(X)
is the poset of locally finite subsets
Z
of
X
.
Here
Z
is locally finite if the induced bornology of
Z
consists of the finite subsets.

2.

Additivity: For any family
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{BC}
the natural map
E(\bigsqcup^{\mathrm{free}}_{i\in I}X_{i})\to\prod_{i\in I}E(X_{i})
(defined using excision and assuming that the product in
{\mathbf{D}}
exists) is an equivalence, where
the free union
\bigsqcup^{\mathrm{free}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
is the bornological coarse space with underlying set
\bigsqcup_{i\in I}X_{i}
with the coarse structure generated by entourages
\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
for families

(U_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\prod_{i\in I}{\mathcal{C}}_{X_{i}}
, and whose bounded subsets are subsets
B
such that

B\cap X_{i}
is bounded for all
i
, and non-empty for at most finitely many
i
in
I
.

3.

Strongness:
E(X)\simeq 0
for weakly flasque bornological coarse spaces, where
X
is called weakly flasque if it admits a non-expanding and shifting (see
Remark

2.22
.
3
) selfmap
f:X\to X
such that

\mathrm{Yo}^{s}(f)\simeq\mathrm{Yo}^{s}({\mathtt{id}}_{X})
, see (
2.12
).

2.3
Local and locally finite homology theories

In this section we recall the notions of a local and a locally finite homology theory from
[
BE20a
,
BE20b
]
.
We further recall the construction via the cone functor of the local homology theory associated to a coarse homology theory and
the implementation of additional support conditions.

We start with the notion of local finiteness which applies to functors defined on spaces equipped with a bornology.
We consider a functor

E:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
or
E:\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{D}}
and assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is stable, complete and cocomplete.

Definition 2.24
.

We say that
E
is locally finite if the canonical map

E(X)\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\mathrm{Cofib}(E(X\setminus B)\to E(X))

(2.14)

is an equivalence for every
X
with bornology
{\mathcal{B}}
.

Equivalently,
E
is locally finite if

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}E(X\setminus B)\simeq 0

for every
X
. The inclusions of locally finite functors into all functors
are the right-adjoints of left Bousfield localizations

\displaystyle(-)^{\mathrm{lf}}:{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathbf{UBC},{\mathbf{D}})\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{lf}}(\mathbf{UBC},{\mathbf{D}}):\mathrm{incl}\ ,

\displaystyle(-)^{\mathrm{lf}}:{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathbf{TB},{\mathbf{D}})\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{lf}}(\mathbf{TB},{\mathbf{D}}):\mathrm{incl}

(2.15)

whose units
E\to E^{\mathrm{lf}}
have the components (
2.14
), see
[
BE20b
, Sec. 7.1.2]
.

Remark 2.25
.

In this remark we recall the characterization of homology theories on the category
{\mathbf{Top}}
of topological spaces.
We let
\ell:{\mathbf{Top}}\to\mathbf{Spc}
be the Dwyer-Kan localization at the weak homotopy equivalences. This is one of the presentations of the category
\mathbf{Spc}
of spaces (anima). Let
E:{\mathbf{Top}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a functor to a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category.
The functor
E
is a homology theory on
{\mathbf{Top}}
if it sends weak homotopy equivalences to equivalences and has the property that the induced factorization
\mathbf{Spc}\to{\mathbf{D}}

\textstyle{{\mathbf{Top}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\ell}
\textstyle{\mathbf{D}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Spc}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

preserves colimits.

By the universal property of
\mathbf{Spc}
the evaluation at
*
is an equivalence

{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathbf{Spc},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{ev}}_{*}}}{{\simeq}}{\mathbf{D}}

so that the category of
{\mathbf{D}}
-valued homology theories is equivalent to
{\mathbf{D}}
itself.
For
D
in
{\mathbf{D}}
we write
D(-)
for the corresponding homology theory
X\mapsto\ell(X)\otimes D

(written using the tensor structure of
{\mathbf{D}}
over
\mathbf{Spc}
).

A homology theory on
{\mathbf{Top}}
in particular has the following properties:

1.

homotopy invariance: The map
E([0,1]\times X)\to E(X)
is an equivalence for every
X
in
{\mathbf{Top}}
.

2.

excisiveness: For every pair of big families
({\mathcal{Y}},{\mathcal{Z}})
of
X
(in the topological sense) covering
X
(i.e., there exists members
Y
and
Z
with
Y\cup Z=X
) we have a push-out square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 21.00696pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-21.00696pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.79167pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 45.79167pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Y}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.40973pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-13.92361pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E({\mathcal{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.00696pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 45.00696pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

Note that these two properties do not suffice to characterize homology theories.
The formulation of excision with big families avoids to talk about the usual additional point-set theoretical assumptions on decompositions of
X
into two subsets. But if, e.g.,
(Y,Z)
is a closed decomposition of a metric space
X
such that the inclusions
Y\to U_{r}[Y]

and
Z\to U_{r}[Z]
are homotopy equivalences for all sufficiently small
r
, then we have a push-out

\textstyle{E(Y\cap Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(X)}

provided
E
is homotopy invariant and excisive.

\blacksquare

Let
E:\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a functor to a cocomplete and complete stable
\infty
-category.

Definition 2.26

(
[
BE20b
, 7.27]
)
.

The functor
E
is a locally finite homology theory if it is

1.

homotopy invariant

2.

excisive

3.

locally finite.

Remark 2.27
.

By
[
BE20b
, 7.1.4]
the composition

{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathbf{Top}},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}(\mathbf{TB},{\mathbf{D}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(-)^{\mathrm{lf}}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{lf}}(\mathbf{TB},{\mathbf{D}})

of the restriction along the forgetful functor
\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{Top}}
and the left-adjoint in (
2.15
)
sends homology theories
E
to locally finite homology theories
E^{\mathrm{lf}}
.
The original homology theory can be recovered from
E^{\mathrm{lf}}
as the homology theory represented by the spectrum
E^{\mathrm{lf}}(*)
.

On the other hand, if
F:\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a locally finite homology theory, then the spectrum
F(*)
represents a cohomology theory
F(*)(-)
and we have a natural transformation of functors
F(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(-)\to F(-):\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{D}}
, which attempts to recover
F
from its value on the point.
By
[
BE20b
, Thm. 7.43]

the map
F(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\to F(X)
is an equivalence for a large class of topological bornological spaces, e.g, for locally finite simplicial complexes with the topology and bornology induced by the spherical path metric
[
BE20b
, Ex. 7.42]
.

\blacksquare

Remark 2.28
.

We say that
E:\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is open (or closed) excisive if

E
sends open (or closed) decompositions to push-out squares.
A locally finite and open (or closed) excisive functor
E:\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{D}}
has an additional contravariant functoriality for topological bornological inclusions
Y\to X
of open (closed) subsets. Indeed, by open (or closed) excision for every closed (or open)
B
in
{\mathcal{B}}\cap Y
we have

\mathrm{Cofib}(E(Y\setminus B)\to E(Y))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Cofib}(E(X\setminus B)\to E(X))\ .

In the limit in (
2.14
) we can restrict to closed (open) bounded subsets contained in
Y
.
By local finiteness get we a map

\displaystyle E(X)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}}\mathrm{Cofib}(E(X\setminus B)\to E(X))\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}\cap Y}\mathrm{Cofib}(E(X\setminus B)\to E(X))

\blacksquare

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}\cap Y}\mathrm{Cofib}(E(Y\setminus B)\to E(Y))\simeq E(Y)\ .

We now consider functors
E:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
to a stable cocomplete
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
. Homotopy invariance is defined as above for
\mathbf{TB}
. We will consider the following additional properties:

1.

excisiveness: For every coarsely and uniformly excisive decomposition
(Y,Z)
of
X

we have a push-out square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.2083pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.2083pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(Y\cap Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.72913pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.72913pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 63.61107pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.63889pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.2083pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.2083pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{E(X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

2.

vanishing on flasques:
E([0,\infty)\otimes X)\simeq 0
for all
X
in
\mathbf{UBC}
.

3.

u
-continuity:
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}\cap{\mathcal{U}}}E(X_{U})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}E(X)
, where

X_{U}
is obtained from
X
by replacing the coarse structure by the smaller coarse structure generated by
U
.

Definition 2.29
.

The functor
E
is called a local homology theory if it has the following properties:

1.

homotopy invariance

2.

excisiveness

3.

vanishing on flasques

4.

u
-continuity.

We have then following constructions of local homology theories:

1.

If
E:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a local homology theory which is coarsening invariant
[
BE20a
, Def. 11.8]
and
{\mathbf{D}}
is complete, then
E^{\mathrm{lf}}
is again a (coarsening invariant, see
[
BE20a
, Lem. 11.10]
) local homology theory which is in addition locally finite.
The assumption on coarsening invariance is needed in order to ensure that
E^{\mathrm{lf}}
is
u
-continuous. Without this assumption we would have the problem of commuting the limit defining
E^{\mathrm{lf}}
with the colimit appearing in the
u
-continuity condition.

2.

If
E:\mathbf{TB}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a locally finite homology theory, then
E\tau:\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{TB}
is a local homology theory
[
BE20a
, Lem. 3.16]
.

3.

If
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a coarse homology theory, then
E\iota:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a local homology theory
[
BE20a
, Lem 3.13]
.

4.

If
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a coarse homology theory which is in addition strong, then

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:=E\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a local homology theory
[
BE20a
, Lem. 9.6]
.
It is coarsening invariant by
[
BE20a
, Ex. 11.9]
.

Example 2.30
.

The cone boundary (
2.9
) together with (
2.13
) induces a natural transformation of local homology theories

a:\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\to E\iota:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}\ .

(2.16)

which we call the index map.
\blacksquare

Note that a local homology theory
E:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
with complete target is not necessarily locally finite.
By
[
BE20a
, Sec. 11]
we have comparison transformations

E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(-)\to E^{\mathrm{lf}}\leftarrow E:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}\ ,

(2.17)

where
E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(-)
denotes the locally finite homology theory represented by the spectrum
E(*)
.

Example 2.31
.

Let
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
be a strong and additive coarse homology theory with complete target. If
X
in
\mathbf{UBC}
is presented by a locally finite finite-dimensional simplicial complex with the spherical path metric, then by
[
BE20a
, Prop. 11.23]
transformations from (
2.17
) induce equivalences

\Sigma E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}(E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty})^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\leftarrow}}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\ .

Here we used the equivalence
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(*)\simeq\Sigma E(*)
.

\blacksquare

Example 2.32
.

Let
X
in
\mathbf{UBC}
be a uniform bornological coarse space whose bornology is the bornology of relatively compact subsets of the underlying topological space. Note that
X
is then necessarily locally compact.

We assume that
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a strong coarse homology theory with complete target
{\mathbf{D}}
.
By the coarsening invariance of
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}

[
BEKW20
, Prop. 9.33]
,
[
BE20a
, Ex. 11.9]
we know that

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
, and therefore also
(E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty})^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
do not depend on the choice of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Since the restrictions the uniform structures as above to a compact subset of
X
is determined by the topology of
X
alone we conclude that
(E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty})^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
does not depend on the uniform structure as well.
So
(E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty})^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
is an invariant of the locally compact topological space
X
.
By
Example

2.31
, if
X
is a locally finite finite-dimensional simplicial complex and
E
is in addition additive, then

(E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty})^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\simeq\Sigma E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
where the right-hand side clearly only depends on the locally compact space
X
.

Assume now that
X
in
\mathbf{UBC}
has the additional structure of a finite-dimensional, locally finite simplicial complex whose simplices are uniformly equicontinuous and coarsely bounded.
Let
X_{\mathrm{simp}}
be
X
equipped with the uniform and coarse structure induced by the spherical path metric
of the simplices. In both cases we assume that the bornologies consist of relatively compact subsets.
Then the identity of the underlying set is a morphism
X_{\mathrm{simp}}\to X
in
\mathbf{UBC}
.
We have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X_{\mathrm{simp}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty})^{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{\mathrm{simp}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Sigma E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(X_{\mathrm{simp}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty})^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)}
\textstyle{\Sigma E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

which presents
\Sigma E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(X)
as a retract of
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
.

\blacksquare

Remark 2.33
.

The functor

\Sigma E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(-):\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}

has the additional contravariant functoriality for inclusions of open subsets. We do not know whether

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(-)
has such a contravariant functoriality.
\blacksquare

Example 2.34
.

The consideration from
Example

2.32
applies in particular to a complete finite-dimensional Riemannian manifold
M
presenting an object of
\mathbf{UBC}
.
Assume that
E
is strong and additive with complete target
{\mathbf{D}}
.
Then

\Sigma E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(M)
is a retract of
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(M)
.
We get a factorization of the index map over a locally finite version
a_{M}^{\mathrm{lf}}

\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{M}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces E(*)^{\mathrm{lf}}(M)}
\scriptstyle{a^{\mathrm{lf}}_{M_{\mathrm{simp}}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(M_{\mathrm{simp}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{a_{M_{\mathrm{simp}}}}
\textstyle{E(M)}

of the index map.

\blacksquare

We now take advantage of the geometric construction of local homology theories via the cone functor in order to add support conditions.
Let
X
be in
\mathbf{UBC}
and
{\mathcal{Y}}
and
{\mathcal{Z}}
be two big families in
X
.

Definition 2.35
.

We define the big families

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}}):={\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{Y}},\qquad{\mathcal{O}}^{-}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\{{{\mathbb{R}}^{-}}\}\times{\mathcal{Y}}

and

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X):=\{{\mathbb{R}}^{-}\}\times{\mathcal{Z}}\cup\{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\}\times X,\qquad{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}}):=}{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)\cap{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}})

in
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
.

Recall
Example

2.4
for the notations in the above definition.

Note that all members of
{\mathcal{O}}^{-}({\mathcal{Y}})
are flasque.

{\mathcal{Z}}
{\mathcal{Y}}
X
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}({\mathcal{Y}})
{\mathcal{O}}^{-}({\mathcal{Y}})
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}})
{\mathcal{O}}^{-}({\mathcal{Y}}\cap{\mathcal{Z}})

We let
\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}
be the category of pairs
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
of
X
in
\mathbf{UBC}
and a big family
{\mathcal{Z}}
. A morphism

f:(X,{\mathcal{Z}})\to(X^{\prime},{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime})
is a morphism
f:X\to X^{\prime}
in
\mathbf{UBC}
such that
f({\mathcal{Z}})\subseteq{\mathcal{Z}}^{\prime}
.
We define the category
\mathbf{BC}^{(2)}
of pairs
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
of bornological coarse spaces with a big family similarly.
We have a forgetful functor
\iota:\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to\mathbf{BC}^{(2)}
and the functor

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{-}:\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to\mathbf{BC}^{(2)}\ ,\quad(X,{\mathcal{Z}})\mapsto({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X),{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X))\ .

If
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a functor to a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category, then we get the functor

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{-}:\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to{\mathbf{D}}\ ,\quad(X,{\mathcal{Z}})\mapsto E({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X))\ .

(2.18)

It comes with a natural transformation
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{-}\to E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}
induced by the maps

{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\infty}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
for every
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
in
\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}
, where we consider
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}
as a functor on
\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}
using the forgetful functor
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})\mapsto X
.

We now assume that
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a strong coarse homology theory.
As discussed above, the induced functor
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}:\mathbf{UBC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a local homology theory.

Definition 2.36
.

We call the functor
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{-}:\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to{\mathbf{D}}
from (
2.18
) the associated local homology theory with support.

Remark 2.37
.

Let
{\mathcal{Z}}
be a big family in
X
.
For a subset
Y
or a big family
{\mathcal{Y}}
on
X
, in order to simplify the notation, we will write

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(Y):=E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{Y\cap{\mathcal{Z}}}(Y)
or
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}}):=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{Y\in{\mathcal{Y}}}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}\cap Y}(Y)
.
This notation is consistent since

E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}})\simeq E({\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}}))
, where
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathcal{Y}})
is defined in
Definition

2.35
.

\blacksquare

Lemma 2.38
.

We have a functorial fibre sequence

E\iota({\mathcal{Z}})\to E{\mathcal{O}}^{{\infty}}_{{{\{\emptyset\}}}}(X)\to E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)\ .

(2.19)

Proof.

This is the Mayer-Vietoris sequence for the decomposition

(\{{\mathbb{R}}^{-}\}\times{\mathcal{Z}},\{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\}\times X)
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}^{\infty}(X)
together with the flasqueness of the members of

\{{\mathbb{R}}^{-}\}\times{\mathcal{Z}}
.
∎

The following lemma says that
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{-}(-)
has the analogues for pairs of the properties of a local homology theory.

Lemma 2.39
.

The functor
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{-}(-):\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to{\mathbf{D}}
has the following properties:

1.

homotopy invariant: For
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
in
\mathbf{UBC}
the projection

([0,1]\otimes X,[0,1]\times{\mathcal{Z}})\to(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
induces an equivalence
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{[0,1]\times{\mathcal{Z}}}([0,1]\otimes X)\to E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)
.

2.

excisiveness: If
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
is in
\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}
and
(A,B)
is a
coarsely and uniformly excisive decomposition of
X
, then the square

\textstyle{E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(A\cap B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)}

is a push-out square.

3.

vanishing on flasques: For every
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
in
\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}
we have
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{Z}}}({\mathbb{R}}\otimes X)\simeq 0
.

4.

u
-continuity: For every
(X,{\mathcal{Z}})
in
\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}
we have
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{U\in{\mathcal{C}}}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X_{U})\simeq E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)\ .

Proof.

One option is to redo the argument of
[
BE20a
, Lem. 9.6]
with the additional support condition.
Alternatively one could employ the fibre sequence (
2.19
) and the fact that
E\iota
and
E{\mathcal{O}}
are known to be local homology theories (see
[
BE20a
, Lem 3.13]
for
E\iota
and
[
BE20a
, Lem. 9.6]
for
E{\mathcal{O}}
).
∎

We now assume that
E:\mathbf{BC}\to{\mathbf{D}}
is a strong coarse homology theory. We incorporate the support conditions into
the index map (
2.16
) as follows. We define the functor

E\iota^{\prime}:\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to{\mathbf{D}}\ ,\quad(X,{\mathcal{Z}})\mapsto E\iota({\mathcal{Z}})\ .

We added the superscript
′
in order to make clear that this functor takes the big family instead of the space.

Definition 2.40
.

The index map

a:\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}_{-}^{\infty}\to E\iota^{\prime}:\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to{\mathbf{D}}

with support conditions is the natural transformation of functors
\mathbf{UBC}^{(2)}\to{\mathbf{D}}
with components

a_{X,{\mathcal{Z}}}:\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)\to E\iota({\mathcal{Z}})

(2.20)

given by

\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{\mathrm{cone}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma^{-1}E(\{{\mathbb{R}}^{-}\}\otimes{\mathcal{Z}}\cup\{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\}\otimes{X})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{MV}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E({\mathcal{Z}})\ .

Here we consider
\{{\mathbb{R}}^{-}\}\otimes{\mathcal{Z}}\cup\{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\}\otimes{X}
as a big family of
{{\mathbb{R}}}\otimes X
,
\partial^{MV}
is the Mayer-Vietoris boundary associated to the decomposition into
(\{{\mathbb{R}}^{-}\}\otimes{\mathcal{Z}},\{{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\}\otimes{X})
, and we use that

the inclusion
X\to{\mathbb{R}}\times X
,
x\mapsto(0,x)
induces an equivalence
E({\mathcal{Z}})\simeq E(\{0\}\times{\mathcal{Z}})
.

Remark 2.41
.

The index map is the boundary map of the fibre sequence (
2.19
).
In particular, we have a fibre sequence

\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\{\emptyset\}}}(X)\to\Sigma^{-1}E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{{\mathcal{Z}}}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a_{X,{\mathcal{Z}}}}}{{\to}}E({\mathcal{Z}}).

(2.21)

\blacksquare

2.4
C^{*}
-algebras and categories and their
K
-theory

In this section we recall some basic constructions from non-commutative homotopy theory.
In particular we recall the
E
- and
K
-theory for graded
C^{*}
-algebra.
Instead of providing explicit cycle-by-relation constructions we will give a complete description
in terms of universal properties. All needed classes will be derived from explicit homomorphisms
between suitable
C^{*}
-algebras (see
Remark

2.48
and
Remark

2.49
), and the boundary operators in long exact sequences
come from general homotopy-theoretic principles (see
Remark

2.42
).

Let
G
be a countable discrete group. By
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we denote the category of not necessarily unital
C^{*}
-algebras with a
G
-action
by automorphisms. The left action of
G
on itself turns the Hilbert space
L^{2}(G)
into a
G
-Hilbert space.
We let
K_{G}
denote the algebra of compact operators on
L^{2}(G)\otimes\ell^{2}
with the induced
G
-action. In the present paper we will need the cases of a trivial group and the two-element group
G=C_{2}
.
The category
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
has a symmetric monoidal structure
\otimes
given by the maximal tensor product.

We will consider the following properties of a functor
F:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
to some cocomplete stable
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
:

1.

homotopy invariance: For every
A
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the inclusion
A\to A\otimes C([0,1])
(induced by the inclusion
{\mathbb{C}}\to C([0,1])
as constant functions) induces an equivalence
F(A)\to F(A\otimes C([0,1]))
.

2.

K_{G}
-stablility:
F
sends
K_{G}
-equivalences to an equivalences. Thereby

f:A\to B
is a
K_{G}
-equivalence if
f\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{K_{G}}:A\otimes K_{G}\to B\otimes K_{G}
is a homotopy equivalence, or equivalently, is sent to an equivalence by every homotopy invariant functor.

3.

exactness:
F(0)\simeq 0
and
F
sends every exact sequence
0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
in

GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
to a fibre sequence
F(A)\to F(B)\to F(C)
in
{\mathbf{D}}
.

4.

s-finitary: For every
A
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the natural morphism induces an equivalence
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{A^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}A}F(A^{\prime})\simeq F(A)
, where
A^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}A
is the poset of
G
-invariant separable subalgebras of
A
.

5.

sum-preserving: For any family
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the canonical map induced by the inclusions
A_{j}\to\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i}
for all
j
in
I
induces an equivalence

\bigoplus_{i\in I}F(A_{i})\simeq F(\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i})
.

Remark 2.42
.

Note that exactness is a property the functor can have or not. If
F
is exact, then the boundary map

\partial:F(C)\to\Sigma F(A)
is implicitly encoded into the functor.

Consider for example the
K
-theory functor
K:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
described further below. This functor
turns out to be exact and therefore sends an exact sequence of
C^{*}
-algebras to a fibre sequence of
KU
-modules. Upon taking homotopy groups we get a long exact sequence

K_{*}(A)\to K_{*}(B)\to K_{*}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\to}}K_{*-1}(A)

of abelian groups.
Classically one considers the graded group-valued functor
K_{*}
only. In order to even state the exactness of the latter one must provide the boundary as an additional datum. In the cycle-by-relation picture
it is given by an explicit formula involving unitaries and projections, and this approach requires various verifications of well-definedness, exactness, and naturality.
Here different authors use different formulas which have to be compared. Eventually, all these constructions,
at least up to sign, provide the boundary map coming from homotopy theory. We refer to the discussion
of this question in
[
BLP23
]
.

\blacksquare

Following
[
Bun23
]
(for
G=e
) and
[
BD24
]
(for general
G
) we adopt the following definition:

Definition 2.43

(
[
BD24
]
)
.

The equivariant
E
-theory functor

\mathrm{e}^{G}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}^{G}

is an initial homotopy invariant,
K_{G}
-stable, exact, sum-preserving and
s
-finitary functor to a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category.

In other words, the equivariant
E
-theory functor is uniquely characterized by the universal property that
\mathrm{E}^{G}
is cocomplete and stable, and that

(\mathrm{e}^{G})^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathrm{E}^{G},{\mathbf{D}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,K_{G},\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{sfin},\oplus}(GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})

is an equivalence for any cocomplete stable
\infty
-category
{\mathbf{D}}
, where the target is the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Fun}}(GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{D}})
of functors having the list of properties indicated by the superscripts.
In the case of
G=\{e\}
we will omit the superscript
G
.

Since for any
A
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
precomposition
with
A\otimes-:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves homotopy invariant,
K_{G}
-stable, exact, sum-preserving and
s
-finitary functors the category
\mathrm{E}^{G}
has furthermore a uniquely determined symmetric monoidal structure such that
\mathrm{e}^{G}
extends to a symmetric monoidal functor.
It turns out that
\mathrm{E}^{G}
is a presentably symmetric monoidal
\aleph_{1}
-presentable stable
\infty
-category
[
BD24
]
.

The tensor unit of
\mathrm{E}
is
\mathrm{e}({\mathbb{C}})
. One can check
[
Bun23
]
that the commutative ring spectrum

KU:={\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{E}}(\mathrm{e}({\mathbb{C}}),\mathrm{e}({\mathbb{C}}))

in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Sp})
is equivalent to the usual complex
K
-theory spectrum.
The symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{Res}_{G}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
equipping
C^{*}
-algebras with the trivial
G
-action descends to a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathrm{Res}_{G}:\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{E}^{G}
. Consequently,
\mathrm{E}^{G}
is enriched over the category
{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
of
KU
-modules.
For
A,B
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we will write

\mathrm{E}^{G}(A,B):={\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{E}^{G}}(\mathrm{e}^{G}(A),\mathrm{e}^{G}(B))

for the bivariant
\mathrm{E}^{G}
-theory
KU
-module of
A,B
. Considering the mapping spectra in
\mathrm{E}^{G}
as
KU
-modules encodes Bott-periodicity in a natural way.

Remark 2.44
.

As shown in
[
BD24
]
, for separable
A,B
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
there is a canonical isomorphism of groups
between
\pi_{0}\mathrm{E}^{G}(A,B)
and the classical equivariant
E
-theory groups
as defined, e.g., in
[
GHT00
]
using homotopy classes of asymptotic morphisms. Furthermore, the restriction of
\mathrm{e}^{G}
to separable
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras yields a
functor
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to(\mathrm{E}^{G})^{\aleph_{1}}
, where
(\mathrm{E}^{G})^{\aleph_{1}}
denotes the full subcategory of
\aleph_{1}
-compact objects in
\mathrm{E}^{G}
. Upon going to the homotopy category this functor is equivalent to the equivariant
E
-theory functor
constructed in
[
GHT00
]
.
This justifies to consider the
functor characterized in
Definition

2.43
as the correct
\infty
-categorical enhancement of classical
E
-theory.
\blacksquare

We next
describe the extension of
E
-theory to graded
C^{*}
-algebras. Our approach is an
\infty
-categorical version of
[
Haa99
]
. The category
(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})^{\mathrm{gr}}
of graded
C^{*}
-algebras is the same as the category
C_{2}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of
C_{2}
-
C^{*}
-algebras, but we equip it with the graded version
\stimes
of the maximal tensor product which involves Koszul sign rules.
One again checks that for any
A
in
C_{2}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
precomposition by the functor
A\stimes-:C_{2}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to C_{2}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves homotopy invariant,
K_{{C_{2}}}
-stable, exact, sum-preserving and
s
-finitary functors. Consequently,
the category
\mathrm{E}^{C_{2}}
has an essentially unique symmetric monoidal structure
\stimes
such that
\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}
has an essentially unique symmetric monoidal refinement with respect to the graded tensor product
\stimes
on
C_{2}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}=(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})^{\mathrm{gr}}
.

We now consider the graded
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathcal{S}}:=C_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
with the grading involution sending
f(x)
to
f(-x)
.
If
A
is a graded
C^{*}
-algebra and
\Phi
is a selfadjoint odd unbounded multiplier of
A
such that
(i+\Psi)^{-1}\in A
, then using function calculus we can define a morphism of graded
C^{*}
-algebras

\Psi_{*}:{\mathcal{S}}\to A\ ,\quad f\mapsto f(\Psi)\ .

(2.22)

We can recover
\Psi
by
\Psi=\Psi_{*}(X)
, where
X
is the multiplier of
{\mathcal{S}}
generating the identity morphism (given by
(Xf)(x):=xf(x)
), and where we implicitly used the extension of
\Psi_{*}
to some unbounded multipliers.
The algebra
{\mathcal{S}}
has a structure of a coalgebra in
(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})^{\mathrm{gr}}
with counit
\epsilon:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
given by
f\mapsto f(0)
(induced by the multiplier
0
on
{\mathbb{C}}
) and the coproduct

\Delta:{\mathcal{S}}\to{\mathcal{S}}\stimes{\mathcal{S}},

(2.23)

induced by the multiplier

X\stimes 1+1\stimes X
(see, e.g.,
[
HG04
]
for a nice description).
Since
\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}
is symmetric monoidal,
\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}})
becomes a coalgebra in
\mathrm{E}^{C_{2}}
.

Definition 2.45
.

We denote by

\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=\mathbf{Comod}_{\mathrm{E}^{C_{2}}}(\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}}))

the presentably symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category
of comodules over the coalgebra
\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}})
in
\mathrm{E}^{C_{2}}
.
We further define the lax symmetric monoidal functor

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}:=\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}})\stimes\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}(-):(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})^{\mathrm{gr}}\to\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{gr}}\ .

Thus by definition, the functor
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}

sends a graded
C^{*}
-algebra
A
to the cofree comodule
\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}})\stimes\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}(A)
over
\mathrm{e}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}})
.
The functor
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{gr}}
is homotopy invariant,
K_{{C_{2}}}
-stable, exact, sum-preserving and s-finitary.
We do not know whether this functor satisfies a universal property.

Remark 2.46
.

In order to see that our definition reproduces the classical
E
-theory groups for graded
C^{*}
-algebras
A,B
we observe (using the universal property of the cofree comodule functor) that

\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{gr}}(A,B)\simeq\mathrm{E}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}}\stimes A,B)\ .

(2.24)

If
A
and
B
are separable, then it follows from
[
Haa99
]
and the fact
[
BD24
]
that the group
\pi_{0}\mathrm{E}^{C_{2}}({\mathcal{S}}\stimes A,B)
is the classical
C_{2}
-equivariant
E
-theory group that

\pi_{0}\mathrm{E}^{\mathrm{gr}}(A,B)
is classical graded
E
-theory group of
A
and
B
.
\blacksquare

Let
\mathrm{triv}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})^{\mathrm{gr}}
be the functor (the same as
\mathrm{Res}_{C_{2}}
) which equips a
C^{*}
-algebra with the trivial
grading. Then we have a commutative diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 30.30276pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-22.60829pt\raise 0.0pt\raise-23.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.10834pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.51112pt\raise-14.94444pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
